=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
The AtCoder company office is represented as a grid of H rows and W columns. Let (i, j) denote the cell at the i-th row from the top and j-th column from the left.
The state of each cell is represented by a character S_{i,j}. If S_{i,j} is #, that cell has a wall; if S_{i,j} is ., that cell is a floor; if S_{i,j} is H, that cell has a humidifier placed on a floor cell.
A certain cell is considered humidified if it can be reached from at least one humidifier cell by at most D moves up, down, left, or right without passing through a wall. Note that any cell with a humidifier is always humidified.
Find the number of humidified floor cells.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W D
S_{1,1}S_{1,2}\cdotsS_{1,W}
S_{2,1}S_{2,2}\cdotsS_{2,W}
\vdots
S_{H,1}S_{H,2}\cdotsS_{H,W}

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq H \leq 1000
- 1 \leq W \leq 1000
- 0 \leq D \leq H\times W
- S_{i,j} is #, ., or H. (1 \leq i \leq H, 1 \leq j \leq W)
- All input numbers are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 4 1
H...
#..H
.#.#

Sample Output 1

5

Five cells (1,1), (1,2), (1,4), (2,3), (2,4) are humidified.

Sample Input 2

5 6 2
##...H
H.....
..H.#.
.HH...
.###..

Sample Output 2

21

Sample Input 3

1 6 3
...#..

Sample Output 3

0

It is possible that no cells are humidified.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

import sys
from collections import deque

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    H, W, D = map(int, input().split())
    grid = []
    q = deque()
    dist = [[-1] * W for _ in range(H)]
    ans = 0
    H_BYTE = b'H'
    WALL = ord('#')
    append = q.append

    for i in range(H):
        row = input().strip()
        grid.append(row)
        pos = row.find(H_BYTE)
        while pos != -1:
            dist[i][pos] = 0
            append(i * W + pos)
            ans += 1
            pos = row.find(H_BYTE, pos + 1)

    if D == 0:
        print(ans)
        return

    popleft = q.popleft
    # maybe local variables
    grid_local = grid
    dist_local = dist
    H_local = H
    W_local = W
    D_local = D
    WALL = 35
    while q:
        v = popleft()
        r = v // W_local
        c = v - r * W_local
        d = dist_local[r][c]
        if d >= D_local:
            continue
        nd = d + 1
        if r > 0:
            nr = r - 1
            if grid_local[nr][c] != WALL and dist_local[nr][c] == -1:
                dist_local[nr][c] = nd
                append(nr * W_local + c)
                ans += 1
        if r + 1 < H_local:
            nr = r + 1
            if grid_local[nr][c] != WALL and dist_local[nr][c] == -1:
                dist_local[nr][c] = nd
                append(nr * W_local + c)
                ans += 1
        if c > 0:
            nc = c - 1
            if grid_local[r][nc] != WALL and dist_local[r][nc] == -1:
                dist_local[r][nc] = nd
                append(r * W_local + nc)
                ans += 1
        if c + 1 < W_local:
            nc = c + 1
            if grid_local[r][nc] != WALL and dist_local[r][nc] == -1:
                dist_local[r][nc] = nd
                append(r * W_local + nc)
                ans += 1

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Full code:

import sys
from collections import deque

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    H, W, D = map(int, input().split())
    H_BYTE = b'H'
    WALL = ord('#')

    if D == 0:
        ans = 0
        for _ in range(H):
            row = input().strip()
            ans += row.count(H_BYTE)
        print(ans)
        return

    grid = []
    q = deque()
    dist = [[-1] * W for _ in range(H)]
    ans = 0
    append = q.append

    for i in range(H):
        row = input().strip()
        grid.append(row)
        pos = row.find(H_BYTE)
        while pos != -1:
            dist[i][pos] = 0
            append(i * W + pos)
            ans += 1
            pos = row.find(H_BYTE, pos + 1)

    popleft = q.popleft
    H_l = H
    W_l = W
    D_l = D
    grid_l = grid
    dist_l = dist

    while q:
        v = popleft()
        r = v // W_l
        c = v - r * W_l
        d = dist_l[r][c]
        if d >= D_l:
            continue
        nd = d + 1

        row = grid_l[r]
        dr = dist_l[r]

        if c > 0:
            nc = c - 1
            if row[nc] != WALL and dr[nc] == -1:
                dr[nc] = nd
                append(r * W_l + nc)
                ans += 1
        if c + 1 < W_l:
            nc = c + 1
            if row[nc] != WALL and dr[nc] == -1:
                dr[nc] = nd
                append(r * W_l + nc)
                ans += 1
        if r > 0:
            nr = r - 1
            row_u = grid_l[nr]
            du = dist_l[nr]
            if row_u[c] != WALL and du[c] == -1:
                du[c] = nd
                append(nr * W_l + c)
                ans += 1
        if r + 1 < H_l:
            nr = r + 1
            row_d = grid_l[nr]
            dd = dist_l[nr]
            if row_d[c] != WALL and dd[c] == -1:
                dd[c] = nd
                append(nr * W_l + c)
                ans += 1

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's consider if there is a possible "distance" issue with BFS and walls: Suppose a cell is reachable from H1 in 2 moves and from H2 in 1 move. Multi-source BFS initializes all H at distance 0. BFS processes in FIFO order. If H1 and H2 both in queue, neighbors at distance1 enqueued. Then distance2 from H1 might be enqueued before H2's distance1? Wait BFS queue order: initial all H. Suppose H1 first, enqueues its neighbors distance1. Then H2, enqueues its neighbors distance1. Then process H1's neighbors distance1, enqueue distance2. Then process H2's neighbors distance1. A cell that is neighbor of H2 distance1 and also distance2 from H1: If H1's distance2 processed before H2's distance1? Let's examine queue order. Initial queue: [H1, H2]. Pop H1, enqueue A (dist1). Queue [H2, A]. Pop H2, enqueue B (dist1) including cell X. Queue [A, B]. Pop A (dist1), enqueue C (dist2) including X? But X already enqueued by B? Wait B is in queue after A? Actually after pop H2, queue [A, B]. Pop A before B, so if A's neighbor X is distance2 from H1, and X is also distance1 from H2 via B, but B hasn't been processed yet. Then A will enqueue X with dist2 before B enqueues X with dist1. Since dist[X] is -1, A sets dist[X]=2. Later B pops, sees dist[X] != -1, skip. This would incorrectly assign distance 2 instead of 1! Is this possible in standard multi-source BFS? Wait, standard BFS with multiple sources: all sources are enqueued initially at distance 0. When processing nodes in FIFO order, all nodes at distance d are processed before nodes at distance d+1. But in the scenario, H1 and H2 are both distance0. A is distance1 from H1. B is distance1 from H2. Queue after processing H1 and H2: [A, B]. A and B are both distance1. If X is distance1 from H2 via B, then X is a neighbor of B. Can X also be distance2 from H1 via A? For X to be neighbor of B (distance1 from H2) and neighbor of A (distance1 from H1), A and B are both at distance1. If X is neighbor of A, then X is distance2 from H1. But if X is also neighbor of B, then X is distance1 from H2. So X is adjacent to both A and B. Queue order [A, B]. Pop A first, enqueue X with dist2. Then pop B, sees X visited, skip. This would be wrong because X should be dist1. Is this a known issue? Let's think carefully: Standard multi-source BFS initializes all sources with distance 0 and enqueues them. Then while queue: pop u, for each neighbor v if not visited, set dist[v]=dist[u]+1, enqueue. This is correct for unweighted graphs. In the scenario, if X is neighbor of B (dist1), then B is distance1 from H2. But B is enqueued after H2 processed. A is enqueued after H1 processed. Both A and B are at distance1. Queue order [A, B] means A was enqueued before B. If A is also distance1, then all distance1 nodes should be processed before distance2 nodes. But A is distance1, so enqueuing X with dist2 is okay because X is a neighbor of a distance1 node. However if X is also neighbor of B, then X is distance1 from H2, but B is a distance1 node that hasn't been processed yet. The algorithm processes A before B, both distance1, so it may set X's distance to 2 before B can set it to 1. Is that possible in standard BFS? Wait, if X is neighbor of B, and B is distance1, then X is at distance2 from H2? No, distance from H2 to X is dist(B)+1 = 2, not 1. I made a mistake: If B is distance1 from H2, then X neighbor of B is distance2 from H2, not distance1. Let's re-evaluate: H2 source distance0. B is a neighbor of H2, so B distance1. Let's update code with id arithmetic. This is faster and cleaner.

BFS:
    while q:
        v = popleft()
        r = v // W_l
        c = v - r * W_l
        d = dist_l[r][c]
        if d >= D_l:
            continue
        nd = d + 1
        row = grid_l[r]
        dr = dist_l[r]

        if c > 0:
            nc = c - 1
            if row[nc] != WALL and dr[nc] == -1:
                dr[nc] = nd
                append(v - 1)
                ans += 1
        if c + 1 < W_l:
            nc = c + 1
            if row[nc] != WALL and dr[nc] == -1:
                dr[nc] = nd
                append(v + 1)
                ans += 1
        if r > 0:
            nr = r - 1
            row_u = grid_l[nr]
            du = dist_l[nr]
            if row_u[c] != WALL and du[c] == -1:
                du[c] = nd
                append(v - W_l)
                ans += 1
        if r + 1 < H_l:
            nr = r + 1
            row_d = grid_l[nr]
            dd = dist_l[nr]
            if row_d[c] != WALL and dd[c] == -1:
                dd[c] = nd
                append(v + W_l)
                ans += 1

This is efficient. Need ensure `v - W_l` corresponds to same column previous row. Since row-major id = r*W + c. Up = (r-1)*W + c = r*W + c - W = v - W. Yes. Down = v + W. Left = v - 1 (same row, c-1). Right = v + 1. Good.

Using `dist = [[-1] * W for _ in range(H)]` memory ~8MB refs + int objects for distances. But int objects for distances are shared among siblings? Let's analyze worst-case memory for dist int objects in a grid BFS. Each popped node with d<D creates `nd = d + 1` object. It may store that object in up to 4 neighbors. If each node has exactly one unvisited neighbor, then each enqueued node's dist reference points to its parent's nd object, which is unique per parent. Number of stored int objects equals number of expanded parents, not number of enqueued nodes. In a path graph, each expanded node has one child, so number of int objects = number of expanded nodes ~1e6. In a full grid, each expanded node (except boundary) has 4 children, but each child's dist reference points to parent's nd object. Number of expanded nodes ~1e6, so int objects ~1e6. So 28MB. Plus refs 8MB. Queue int objects: Each enqueued node id is a Python int. In a full grid, queue size can be large. But note ids are also computed as `v - 1`, `v + 1`, `v - W`, `v + W`; these are new int objects. They are stored in deque. When popped, the deque still holds reference until popleft removes; after popleft, the int object may be freed if no other refs. But queue can hold many. Max queue size in grid BFS is roughly perimeter, maybe O(H+W) for single source? Actually for BFS in a grid, queue size can be O(min(H,W)*? )? Let's think. For a 1000x1000 grid, BFS queue max maybe around 1000? Actually BFS frontier length can be O(H+W) for a single source? For a grid, the number of nodes at distance d is up to 2d+1, but bounded by perimeter; max frontier size is O(min(H,W))? Wait for a full grid, BFS from a corner, frontier at distance d is a diagonal of length d+1, max ~1000. From center, frontier is diamond perimeter, max ~2000. So queue max O(H+W) ~2000, not 1e6! Is that true? In BFS, queue contains nodes at current and next distance. For grid, frontier size is O(H+W) for single source. But with multiple sources, initial queue can be up to 1e6 if all H. If many H, queue initial large. But if all H, no further enqueues. Worst-case queue memory maybe if many sources and many frontier nodes? Could be large but maybe not 1e6? If H cells are scattered, initial q large plus frontier. But q max can be O(HW) if all cells are H? Initial q 1e6, but then no frontier. If half H, initial q 500k, frontier maybe? Could be large. But queue int objects memory maybe up to 1e6. So memory still okay.

If using `array('i')` for dist, memory 4MB, but access slower. Could also use `list` of `array('i')`? Let's see if we should switch to `array` to be safe. AtCoder Python with 1e6 list-of-lists is common and accepted. The problem constraints H,W<=1000, memory likely 1024 MB. No need.

Potential issue: If D is 0 and row.count H, but if row is b'H'*1000, ans1000. Good.

Potential issue
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
.</think>```python
import sys
from collections import deque

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    H, W, D = map(int, input().split())
    H_BYTE = b'H'
    WALL = ord('#')

    if D == 0:
        ans = 0
        for _ in range(H):
            row = input().strip()
            ans += row.count(H_BYTE)
        print(ans)
        return

    grid = []
    q = deque()
    dist = [[-1] * W for _ in range(H)]
    ans = 0
    append = q.append

    for i in range(H):
        row = input().strip()
        grid.append(row)
        pos = row.find(H_BYTE)
        while pos != -1:
            dist[i][pos] = 0
            append(i * W + pos)
            ans += 1
            pos = row.find(H_BYTE, pos + 1)

    popleft = q.popleft
    H_l = H
    W_l = W
    D_l = D
    grid_l = grid
    dist_l = dist

    while q:
        v = popleft()
        r = v // W_l
        c = v - r * W_l

        dr = dist_l[r]
        d = dr[c]
        if d >= D_l:
            continue

        nd = d + 1
        row = grid_l[r]

        if c > 0:
            nc = c - 1
            if row[nc] != WALL and dr[nc] == -1:
                dr[nc] = nd
                append(v - 1)
                ans += 1

        if c + 1 < W_l:
            nc = c + 1
            if row[nc] != WALL and dr[nc] == -1:
                dr[nc] = nd
                append(v + 1)
                ans += 1

        if r > 0:
            nr = r - 1
            row_u = grid_l[nr]
            du = dist_l[nr]
            if row_u[c] != WALL and du[c] == -1:
                du[c] = nd
                append(v - W_l)
                ans += 1

        if r + 1 < H_l:
            nr = r + 1
            row_d = grid_l[nr]
            dd = dist_l[nr]
            if row_d[c] != WALL and dd[c] == -1:
                dd[c] = nd
                append(v + W_l)
                ans += 1

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
